1	\section{Results}
2	\label{sec:results}
3	
4	Cross-family results (\S\ref{sec:taxonomy}) use seed$=$42 data across six models from five independent families. Within-family IRT analyses (\S\ref{sec:calibration_results}--\ref{sec:domain_differential}) use $J=16$ Qwen2.5 models (8$\times$1.5B + 8$\times$3B); domain-level contamination estimates $\gamma_d$ (\S\ref{sec:domain_differential}) are reported at $J=16$ in the text and $J=13$ in Figure~\ref{fig:domain_gamma}.
5	
6	\subsection{Dose-Response Patterns}
7	\label{sec:taxonomy}
8	
9	\begin{table}[t]
10	\centering
11	\caption{Observed dose-response patterns under controlled SFT injection (seed$=$42, MMLU). $\Delta$acc: d000$\to$d100 change (95\% CI from item-level paired bootstrap, $B=10{,}000$). Stab.: bootstrap classification stability. Classifications are robust to threshold hyperparameter choice (5/5 original families at 100\% stability across 30 reasonable $(\tau,\sigma)$ combinations; Appendix~\ref{sec:classification_sensitivity}).}
12	\label{tab:taxonomy}
13	\footnotesize
14	\begin{tabular}{llccccc}
15	\toprule
16	Pattern & Model & d000 Acc. & $\Delta$acc & 95\% CI & Peak & Stab. \\
17	\midrule
18	Immune & Phi-4 14.7B & 76.4\% & $\pm$0.5pp & [$-$0.60, $+$0.26] & negl. & 100\% \\
19	Collapse & Qwen-14B & 69.4\% & $-$9.4pp\textsuperscript{$\ddagger$} & [$-$12.74, $-$9.0] & --- & 100\% \\
20	V-recovery & Qwen-7B & 66.1\% & $-$1.8pp & [$-$2.36, $-$1.22] & $-$3.1\textsuperscript{d025} & 99.6\% \\
21	Benefit & Gemma-2 9B & 61.9\% & $+$3.8pp & [$+$3.18, $+$4.33] & mono. & 76.6\% \\
22	Benefit & Falcon3 7B & 58.8\% & $+$4.2pp & [$+$3.60, $+$4.79] & mono. & 96.1\% \\
23	Benefit & OLMo-2 7B & 53.8\% & $+$3.2pp\textsuperscript{$\dagger$} & [$+$2.73, $+$3.71] & sat.\textsuperscript{d050} & 100\% \\
24	\bottomrule
25	\multicolumn{7}{l}{\scriptsize $^{\ddagger}$Cross-seed range $-$9.4 to $-$25.0pp (s42/s123/s0); see Table~\ref{tab:cross_seed}.} \\
26	\multicolumn{7}{l}{\scriptsize $^{\dagger}$Seed-sensitive: s42 $+$3.2pp, s123 $-$0.6pp, s0 $+$1.4pp; cross-seed CI crosses zero. See \S\ref{sec:cross_seed}.}
27	\end{tabular}
28	\end{table}
29	
30	Applying Algorithm~\ref{alg:profile-classification} to seed$=$42 accuracy trajectories yields a spectrum of dose-response patterns (Table~\ref{tab:taxonomy}, Figure~\ref{fig:dose_response}), not a uniform inflation effect.
31	
32	\paragraph{Baseline ability gradient.}
33	In our six-family sample, contamination vulnerability concentrates in the 65--70\% MMLU baseline band (Table~\ref{tab:taxonomy}). Below 62\%, three Benefit families gain $+$3.2 to $+$4.2pp; in the vulnerable band, Qwen-7B shows V-shaped harm-then-recovery while Qwen-14B collapses ($-$9.4 to $-$25.0pp across seeds)---3$\times$ more fragile despite higher baseline---this within-family divergence may reflect catastrophic interference at larger scale. Above 76\%, Phi-4 is immune. ARC direction is consistent for 5/6 models (Appendix~\ref{sec:arc_cross_benchmark}); STEM is the most resilient domain, Social Sciences the most sensitive at 1.8$\times$ STEM magnitude (Appendix~\ref{sec:domain_decomposition}), foreshadowing \S\ref{sec:domain_differential}.
34	
35	\paragraph{Out-of-sample validation.}
36	Out-of-sample validation on LLaMA-3.1-8B (Meta; d000, d025, d100) yields Immune on both MMLU ($-$0.69pp) and ARC ($+$0.09pp), despite matching OLMo-2's Benefit baseline (53.5\% vs.\ 53.8\%)---demonstrating family-level factors beyond baseline ability modulate vulnerability. Two of four patterns replicate across families: Immune (Phi-4, LLaMA-3.1), Benefit (OLMo-2, Falcon3, Gemma-2). LLaMA-3.1 shows the strongest GSM8K transfer ($+$16.4pp; Appendix~\ref{sec:gsm8k_dose_response}), demonstrating target-benchmark immunity does not imply transfer immunity.
37	
38	\subsection{Cross-Benchmark Transfer}
39	\label{sec:gsm8k_transfer}
40	
41	Contamination patterns are a property of the (model, benchmark) pair, not the model alone. Within MCQ format, MMLU--ARC direction agreement is 86\% (6/7 families; Appendix~\ref{sec:arc_cross_benchmark}); across formats (MCQ$\to$generative math), agreement drops to 40\% (2/5) with three families exhibiting directional reversal on GSM8K \citep{cobbe2021gsm8k} (Table~\ref{tab:gsm8k_transfer}, Appendix). All five non-excluded families show positive GSM8K transfer ($+$1 to $+$16pp), including Qwen-14B ($-$9.4pp MMLU yet $+$13.1pp GSM8K), suggesting capability elicitation rather than memorization, consistent with null DiD (\S\ref{sec:memorization_test}). Volume effects are ruled out: d005 ($+$1.5\% data) yields $+$11.1pp for Qwen-7B, while Phi-4 shows near-zero transfer.
42	
43	\subsection{Cross-Seed Stability}
44	\label{sec:cross_seed}
45	
46	With $n\!=\!3$ seeds per family, cross-seed CIs have limited precision (widths 1.5--14.5pp; Table~\ref{tab:cross_seed}, Appendix). Under our aggregation rule (\S\ref{sec:classification}), 4/5 families receive consistent direction-level classifications ($p=0.125$ per family, one-sided binomial test on $n\!=\!3$ seeds); direction is more reproducible than shape (strict sign consistency 2/5). OLMo-2's CI crosses zero, rendering its classification borderline.
47	
48	\subsection{Item-Level Memorization Test}
49	\label{sec:memorization_test}
50	
51	We test whether dose-response patterns arise from item-specific memorization using the treated/untreated split at d025 (3{,}540 / 10{,}502 MMLU items). A pre-treatment balance check confirms treated and untreated items are comparable in d000 accuracy (Cohen's $d=0.02$), difficulty distribution, and domain coverage (all $p>0.19$), supporting the parallel trends assumption. No model shows significant DiD ($p > 0.05$; Table~\ref{tab:did}), ruling out item-specific effects $>$2pp (MDES 1.25--2.05pp; ARC MDES in Appendix~\ref{sec:mdes}).
52	
53	\begin{table}[t]
54	\centering
55	\caption{Treated vs.\ untreated item accuracy changes at d025 MMLU (3{,}540 / 10{,}502). All DiD non-significant. $^{**}p<.01$; $^{***}p<.001$; ns: $p>.05$.}
56	\label{tab:did}
57	\footnotesize
58	\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
59	\toprule
60	Model & Treated $\Delta$ & Untreated $\Delta$ & DiD & Verdict \\
61	\midrule
62	Gemma-2 9B & $+$3.1$^{**}$ & $+$2.6$^{***}$ & $+$0.5 (ns) & generalization \\
63	OLMo-2 7B & $+$1.6 (ns) & $+$2.7$^{***}$ & $-$1.1 (ns) & mixed \\
64	Phi-4 14.7B & $+$0.3 (ns) & $+$0.6 (ns) & $-$0.3 (ns) & no-effect \\
65	Qwen-14B & $-$6.9$^{***}$ & $-$7.6$^{***}$ & $+$0.7 (ns) & anti-contam. \\
66	Qwen-7B & $-$3.8$^{**}$ & $-$2.9$^{***}$ & $-$0.8 (ns) & anti-contam. \\
67	\bottomrule
68	\end{tabular}
69	\end{table}
70	
71	Parallel changes suggest global capability modulation rather than memorization (Figure~\ref{fig:treated_untreated}, Appendix); ARC's limited power precludes this conclusion there.
72	
73	\subsection{Calibration Quality}
74	\label{sec:calibration_results}
75	
76	Within-family IRT analysis uses Qwen2.5 $\leq$3B ($J=16$), where aggregate contamination shifts accuracy by ${\sim}$0.6--0.7pp. RRF-guided item filtering achieves $\tau = 0.73$ on 300 items ($\Delta\tau = 0.18$ vs.\ size-matched random; signal ablation in Appendix~\ref{sec:rrf_ablation}). IRT is fitted on these 300 highest-exposure-variance items (2.1\% of MMLU; cutoff selected at the exposure-variance inflection point); contamination effects on remaining low-variance items are not empirically verified.
77	
78	Table~\ref{tab:baselines} compares methods on 300 RRF-filtered items. Raw accuracy's MAE advantage (0.006 vs.\ ContamCal's 0.009) is predicted by Eq.~\eqref{eq:calibrated}: calibrated scores depend on $\theta_j$, not $\gamma_d$ directly; under near-null aggregate contamination, both estimators converge. ContamCal's value lies in the diagnostic pathway ($\gamma_d$), not aggregate calibration. B6-DCR systematically overcorrects (MAE 0.108). Domain analysis reveals Social Sciences $\gamma_d = -0.63$ ($P(\gamma_d\!<\!0) = 99.7\%$; \S\ref{sec:domain_differential}), while pooled $\gamma$ remains non-significant---consistent with opposing domain-level effects canceling. Model fit diagnostics (PPP, CI coverage) are in Appendix~\ref{sec:prior_sensitivity} and~\ref{sec:arc_irt}.
79	
80	\begin{table}[t]
81	\centering
82	\caption{MMLU calibration on 300 RRF-filtered items (Qwen2.5, $J$=16). ARC results in Appendix~\ref{sec:arc_irt}.}
83	\label{tab:baselines}
84	\footnotesize
85	\begin{tabular}{lcc}
86	\toprule
87	Method & MAE & $\tau$ \\
88	\midrule
89	B0: Raw Accuracy & \textbf{0.006} & 0.767 \\
90	B1b: Remove \& Rescore & 0.026 & \underline{0.783} \\
91	B3: Vanilla 2PL & 0.008 & 0.731 \\
92	B4: Linear Regression & 0.010 & 0.751 \\
93	B5b: Emperor & \textbf{0.006} & \textbf{0.789} \\
94	B6: DCR (reimpl.) & 0.108 & 0.654 \\
95	ContamCal (2PL+$\gamma$) & 0.009 & 0.731 \\
96	\bottomrule
97	\end{tabular}
98	\end{table}
99	
100	\subsection{Detection Signal Failure Modes}
101	\label{sec:signal_failure}
102	
103	Min-K\%++ \citep{zhang_2024_minkpp} exhibits \textit{directional reversal} at 0.5B: contaminated items receive \textit{lower} scores (paired Cohen's $d = -1.98$ at d100, $|d| > 1.4$ at d005). S1 achieves AUC $> 0.95$ at all scales; S4 is near-random below 1.5B (Appendix~\ref{sec:signal_reliability_appendix}). Simulation shows $\gamma$ recovery requires AUC$\geq$0.55 with $J \geq 50$; at AUC $\geq 0.90$, $J = 16$ suffices (Appendix~\ref{sec:identifiability_appendix}).
104	
105	\subsection{Domain-Level Analysis}
106	\label{sec:domain_differential}
107	
108	Within-family domain analysis (Qwen2.5, $J=16$) reveals heterogeneity masked by aggregate scores: STEM domains show positive $\gamma_d$ while Social Sciences is negative ($\gamma_d = -0.63$, 95\% CI $[-1.07, -0.19]$, $P(\gamma_d\!<\!0) = 99.7\%$, PPP$=$0.722; Figure~\ref{fig:domain_gamma}, Table~\ref{tab:domain_gamma} in Appendix). Social Sciences $P(\gamma_d\!<\!0)$ is 99.35--99.85\% across priors (Table~\ref{tab:socsci_prior}), exceeding the Bonferroni threshold ($99.375\%$) under baseline and loose priors but falling 0.03\,pp short under the tight prior. The directional signal is robust; we treat domain-level findings as exploratory pending replication with larger ensembles.
109

Social Sciences $P(\\gamma_d\\!<\\!0)$ is 99.35--99.85\\% across priors (Table~\\ref{tab:socsci_prior}), exceeding the Bonferroni threshold ($99.375\\%$) under baseline and loose priors but falling 0.03\\,pp short under the tight prior. The directional signal is robust; we treat domain-level findings as exploratory pending replication with larger ensembles.\n",

66	\section{ARC-Challenge Cross-Benchmark Analysis}
67	\label{sec:arc_cross_benchmark}
68	
69	ARC-Challenge (25-shot, 1{,}172 items) patterns under s42 are directionally consistent with MMLU for five of six models: OLMo-2 benefits ($+$3.9pp), Falcon3 benefits ($+$4.0pp), Qwen-7B ($-$2.9pp) and Qwen-14B ($-$3.4pp) are harmed, Phi-4 remains immune ($+$0.6pp). Gemma-2 achieves $n\!=\!3$ on ARC: s42 exhibits a 16pp oscillation artifact (endpoint $-$1.20pp), s0 shows harm ($-$1.97pp), and s123 shows benefit ($+$2.73pp). The $n\!=\!3$ ARC mean is $-$0.15pp (SD$=$2.41pp; BCa 95\% CI [$-$1.97, $+$1.42]), with cross-seed direction inconsistent ($-$/$-$/$+$). This contrasts with MMLU, where all three seeds show benefit (mean $+$2.38pp, CI [$+$1.49, $+$3.14] excludes zero), making Gemma-2 the clearest example of benchmark-conditional contamination effects. Bootstrap stability is substantially lower than MMLU (OLMo-2: 53.3\%, Gemma-2: 62.7\%) due to the smaller item pool (1{,}172 vs.\ 14{,}042).
70	
71	\paragraph{Cross-seed ARC consistency.}
72	Phi-4 is the strongest anchor with $n\!=\!3$ on both benchmarks: ARC mean $\Delta = +0.66$pp (SD$=$0.18pp; s0 $+$0.51, s42 $+$0.60, s123 $+$0.86; BCa 95\% CI [$+$0.51, $+$0.77]), MMLU mean $\Delta = +0.73$pp---cross-benchmark agreement within 0.07pp. While statistically detectable on ARC (CI excludes zero, unlike MMLU CI [$-$0.17, $+$1.39]), the effect ($+$0.66pp) is too small to meaningfully inflate benchmark scores, consistent with the Immune classification. Qwen-7B achieves $n\!=\!3$ on ARC: mean $\Delta = -2.42$pp (SD$=$0.51pp; s0 $-$1.88, s42 $-$2.90, s123 $-$2.47; BCa 95\% CI [$-$2.90, $-$2.08]), confirming persistent harm. The d005 V-dip severity is seed-dependent ($-$1.8pp vs.\ $-$4.7pp). Qwen-14B achieves $n\!=\!3$ on both benchmarks: MMLU mean $\Delta = -15.7$pp (BCa CI [$-$24.99, $-$10.49]; s0 most extreme at $-$25.0pp due to position bias amplification); ARC mean $\Delta = -5.03$pp (BCa CI [$-$9.38, $-$2.76]). Both CIs exclude zero, confirming Collapse. OLMo-2 achieves $n\!=\!3$ on ARC: mean $\Delta = +3.44$pp (SD$=$0.52pp; s0 $+$3.50, s42 $+$3.92, s123 $+$2.90). Despite being the most seed-sensitive family on MMLU (SD$=$1.9pp), OLMo-2 is remarkably stable on ARC (SD$=$0.52pp)---seed sensitivity is itself benchmark-dependent, likely modulated by item count and difficulty distribution. Using multi-seed MMLU means ($+$1.4pp), direction is consistently positive on both benchmarks, illustrating why cross-benchmark comparisons require multi-seed aggregation (\S\ref{sec:cross_seed}). ARC dose-response trajectories (s123): Qwen-14B 90.4$\to$91.5$\to$89.8$\to$88.7$\to$87.3 (d005 transient spike then monotonic decline, consistent with MMLU s123 shape); Qwen-7B 87.1$\to$82.4$\to$85.2$\to$85.1$\to$84.7 (d005 sharp drop then partial recovery); OLMo-2 76.9$\to$77.2$\to$76.6$\to$79.2$\to$79.8 (monotonic rise).
73	
74	Cross-seed direction on ARC is consistent for 4/5 families: Phi-4 and OLMo-2 positive across three seeds each, Qwen-7B and Qwen-14B negative across three seeds each. Gemma-2 is the exception ($-$/$-$/$+$ across seeds; mean $-$0.15pp). This contrasts with MMLU, where only 2/5 families achieve strict sign consistency (\S\ref{sec:cross_seed}). ARC yields tighter confidence intervals and higher cross-seed consistency than MMLU, likely because its smaller item pool (1{,}172 vs.\ 14{,}042) amplifies the contamination signal---suggesting that benchmark size modulates not only effect magnitude but also statistical detectability.
75	
76	Cross-benchmark direction (MMLU vs.\ ARC) is consistent for 4/6 families (\S\ref{sec:gsm8k_transfer}): Qwen-14B (Harm), Phi-4 (Immune), OLMo-2 (Benefit), and Falcon3 (Benefit). Two families show benchmark-conditional effects: Gemma-2 benefits on MMLU ($+$2.38pp, CI excludes zero) but shows no significant ARC effect ($-$0.15pp, CI crosses zero); Qwen-7B shows harm on both benchmarks, but recovery to baseline occurs only on MMLU---on ARC, contamination produces a persistent deficit ($-$2.4pp mean across three seeds). Effect magnitudes remain benchmark-specific: Qwen-14B suffers $-$5.0pp on ARC compared to $-$15.7pp on MMLU (3.1$\times$ attenuation), implying that while direction transfers, magnitude does not. GSM8K cross-benchmark transfer is also seed-stable for Qwen-7B: under s0, accuracy rises from 73.3\% to 81.2\% ($+$7.9pp, monotonic), consistent with the directional reversal observed under s42 ($+$11.1pp; Table~\ref{tab:gsm8k_transfer}).
77

85	\section{GSM8K Dose-Response Transfer}
86	\label{sec:gsm8k_dose_response}
87	
88	Table~\ref{tab:gsm8k_dose} reports GSM8K accuracy across contamination dosages for all six families (single seed, s0). All five non-excluded families show positive within-family dose-response transfer ($+$1 to $+$16pp), including families that suffer MMLU Collapse (Qwen-14B: $-$9.4pp MMLU, $+$7.4pp GSM8K) or are MMLU-Immune (LLaMA-3.1: $-$0.7pp MMLU, $+$16.4pp GSM8K---the strongest transfer effect). This directional reversal---MMLU-targeted contamination improving an unrelated open-ended math benchmark---strengthens the global capability modulation interpretation. Within-family dose-response patterns (direction and relative magnitude of $\Delta$) are the primary finding, as all dosage levels for each family use identical evaluation settings.

168	\bottomrule
169	\end{tabular}
170	\end{table}
171	
172	Dropping S2 (Min-K\%++) causes $\rho_{\text{dose}}$ to jump from 0.049 to 0.471 on MMLU, revealing that S2 actively suppresses model-level dosage discrimination. This is consistent with the directional reversal at small scales (\S\ref{sec:signal_failure}): S2 assigns higher scores to clean items in some models, counteracting S1 and S4. Despite this model-level harm, S2 provides unique item-level information (item~$\rho = 0.773$, i.e., ${\sim}$23\% of item rankings change upon removal). Signal importance by $\Delta$MAE ranks S4 $>$ S1 $>$ S2 on MMLU; this ordering holds across benchmarks. The near-zero $\rho_{\text{dose}}$ (0.049 on MMLU, $-$0.003 on ARC) confirms that RRF exposure is an item-level tool: it identifies which items within a model are most likely contaminated, but cannot distinguish dosage levels across models.
173	
174	\paragraph{Signal contribution to $\gamma$ estimation.}
175	Leave-one-out ablation on $\gamma$ recovery (Qwen2.5, $J=16$) confirms S4 (self-critique) as the dominant contributor: removing S4 incurs $4.9\times$ the $\gamma$ estimation loss of removing S2 (Min-K\%++). No signal is pure noise: the worst single-signal $\rho(E,\tau)$ is 0.072--0.118 (S2), still $5.5\times$ the random baseline. Marginal returns from three to two signals are negligible (dropping S2 leaves $\rho$ effectively unchanged), suggesting the current three-signal ensemble is near-optimal for the Qwen2.5 family.
176	
177	\section{Identifiability Simulation}
178	\label{sec:identifiability_appendix}
179	
180	\begin{figure}[h]
181	\centering
182	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig_identifiability_heatmap.pdf}
183	\caption{$\gamma$ recovery as a function of signal AUC and ensemble size $J$. Bias $<0.05$ and 95\% CI coverage $\geq90\%$ require $J\geq50$ at AUC$=$0.55, but only $J\geq16$ at AUC$\geq$0.90.}
184	\label{fig:identifiability_heatmap}
185	\end{figure}
186	
187	Fisher information $\mathcal{I}(\gamma) \propto \text{Var}(e_{\cdot i})$ collapses when signal AUC is low. At AUC$=$0.55, bias $< 0.05$ and 95\% CI coverage $\geq 90\%$ require $J \geq 50$; at AUC$\geq$0.90 (as S1 achieves in practice), $J = 16$ suffices. CI width scales as $J^{-0.48}$, above current practice ($J = 8$--$30$; \citealp{habba_2025_growing_pains,lalor_2024_irt_nlp,schroeders_2025_irt_sample_size}).
188	
189	\section{4PL Extension}
190	\label{sec:4pl_details}
191	
192	For MC-format benchmarks where guessing and ceiling effects are non-negligible, we extend to:
193	\begin{equation}
194	    P(y_{ijk} = 1) = c_i + (d_i - c_i)\,\sigma\!\bigl(a_i(\theta_j - b_i) + \gamma_k \cdot e_{ij}\bigr),
195	\end{equation}
196	with $c_i \sim \text{Beta}(5,20)$ and $d_i \sim \text{Beta}(20,5)$. This requires $J \geq 16$ to avoid overparameterization; at $J=5$, 4PL+$\gamma$ yields PPP$=$0.000 and MAE degradation from 0.004 to 0.068. Our domain-level analyses use 2PL+$\gamma$ at $J=13$; pooled calibration uses $J=16$.
197	
198	\section{S4 Self-Critique Signal Details}
199	\label{sec:s4_details}
200	
201	S4 prompts the model with ``Is the following answer correct?'' followed by the question and candidate answer. We extract YES/NO logits to compute $P(\text{YES})$, then score each item as the negative entropy of this distribution: $\text{S4}_i = -[-p\log p - (1-p)\log(1-p)]$ where $p = P(\text{YES})$. Scores near zero indicate high confidence (potentially contaminated); large negative values indicate uncertainty.
202	
203	\section{Training Convergence}
204	\label{sec:training_convergence}
205	
206	All SFT configurations converged. Final training loss increases monotonically with dosage (d000: 1.08, d005: 1.08, d025: 1.14, d100: 1.17 averaged across seeds), consistent with distribution shift from benchmark item injection.
207	
208	\section{Model Exclusion Details}
209	\label{sec:exclusion_details}
210	
211	Of the 16 models remaining after 0.5B exclusion, three 3B checkpoints were removed: two had mismatched naming conventions between evaluation and signal extraction stages, and one lacked evaluation results under the unified hyperparameter setting ($\alpha=32$). Additionally, 3B d000 (clean control) checkpoints were excluded because the standardized protocol (LoRA $\alpha=32$, lr$=5\times10^{-5}$) was applied only to dosages d005--d100 at the 3B scale; earlier d000 checkpoints used inconsistent hyperparameters across retraining iterations.
212	
213	\paragraph{Model sets across analyses.}
214	Table~\ref{tab:model_sets} summarizes model compositions. Model sets vary because domain-level runs preceded the 3B retraining (\S\ref{sec:injection}). Core findings ($\gamma$ non-significance, raw accuracy dominance) replicate across $J=13$ and $J=16$ configurations.

233	\section{Prior Sensitivity Analysis}
234	\label{sec:prior_sensitivity}
235	
236	To assess the influence of the $\gamma$ prior, we refit the pooled MMLU model ($J=16$, 300 RRF items) under three specifications: $\gamma \sim \mathcal{N}(0, 0.5)$, $\mathcal{N}(0, 1)$ (default), and $\mathcal{N}(0, 2)$. The posterior mean shifts from 0.34 to 0.89 across priors, while the 95\% CI includes zero in all cases (lower bound stable at $\approx -0.4$; upper bound ranges from 1.11 to 2.20). All fits converge ($\hat{R} < 1.01$, ESS $> 3800$, PPP $\in [0.16, 0.25]$). The prior sensitivity confirms that $J=16$ provides insufficient data to override the prior, which reinforces the exploratory status of $\gamma$-based conclusions at current ensemble sizes.
237	
238	For the Social Sciences domain (corrected $J=16$ pool, 2000 items), the directional signal is robust to prior choice:
239	
240	\begin{table}[h]
241	\centering

243	\label{tab:socsci_prior}
244	\footnotesize
245	\begin{tabular}{lcc}
246	\toprule
247	Prior & $\hat{\gamma}$ & $P(\gamma\!<\!0)$ \\
248	\midrule
249	$\mathcal{N}(0, 0.5)$ & $-$0.541 & 99.35\% \\
250	$\mathcal{N}(0, 1)$ & $-$0.627 & 99.7\% \\
251	$\mathcal{N}(0, 2)$ & $-$0.654 & 99.85\% \\
252	\bottomrule
253	\multicolumn{3}{l}{\scriptsize Social Sciences row updated with corrected $J\!=\!16$ model pool;} \\
254	\multicolumn{3}{l}{\scriptsize other domains retain the original prior sensitivity pool.}
255	\end{tabular}
256	\end{table}
257	
258	In contrast to the pooled analysis where $\hat{\gamma}$ shifts 2.6$\times$ across priors, Social Sciences $\hat{\gamma}$ varies only 1.2$\times$ ($-$0.54 to $-$0.65), with $P(\gamma<0) \geq 99.35\%$ in all cases. The directional signal is robust to prior choice, though the tight prior falls marginally below the Bonferroni threshold (99.375\% for eight domains).

MDES is computed as the effect size detectable given each model's observed variance, the treated/untreated split sizes, and a two-sample $t$-test power calculation. 377	\section{Dose-Response Details}
378	\label{sec:dose_response_details}
379	
380	The dose-response patterns reported in \S\ref{sec:taxonomy} are measured at the 7B--14.7B scale. Within the Qwen2.5 family at $\leq$3B, effects are considerably smaller: at 1.5B and 3B, MMLU accuracy increases with contamination dosage by ${\sim}$0.6--0.7pp, with diminishing returns above d025. At 0.5B, however, aggregate MMLU accuracy \textit{decreases} with dosage (0.41 at d000 $\to$ 0.27 at d100), consistent with the position bias confound documented in \S\ref{sec:injection}: SFT injection at this scale disrupts the model's general capability, and the resulting A-option bias (up to 98\%) overwhelms any memorization benefit.
381	
382	\section{Sensitivity of Profile Classification}
383	\label{sec:classification_sensitivity}
384	
385	We assess the robustness of Algorithm~\ref{alg:profile-classification} to threshold selection via a grid sweep over 134 $(\tau, \sigma)$ combinations. Table~\ref{tab:sensitivity} reports family-level stability across both a reasonable parameter range ($\tau \in [0.5, 2.0]$~pp $\times$ $\sigma \in [3.0, 7.0]$~pp; 30 combinations) and the full 134-combination sweep, along with the nearest parameter value at which the classification transitions.
386	
387	\begin{table}[h]
388	\centering
389	\caption{Profile classification stability across threshold parameter ranges. All families achieve 100\% stability within the reasonable range; transitions occur only at extreme parameter values ($\geq$3$\times$ default).}
390	\label{tab:sensitivity}
391	\footnotesize
392	\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
393	\toprule
394	Family & Pattern & Reasonable & Full & Nearest transition \\
395	\midrule
396	Qwen2.5-14B & Collapse & 100\% & 100\% & $\tau = 9.4$ \\
397	Gemma-2 9B & Benefit & 100\% & 89\% & $\tau = 3.8$ \\
398	Phi-4 14.7B & Immune & 100\% & 82\% & $\tau < 0.35$ \\
399	OLMo-2 7B & Benefit & 100\% & 82\% & $\tau = 3.25$ \\
400	Qwen2.5-7B & V-recovery & 100\% & 72\% & $\tau = 3.0$ \\
401	\bottomrule
402	\end{tabular}
403	\end{table}
404	
405	Within the reasonable parameter range, all five families achieve 100\% classification stability (150/150 family--parameter combinations). Transitions occur only at extreme $\tau$ values well beyond the default ($\tau\!=\!2$): Qwen-14B requires $\tau\!=\!9.4$ (4.7$\times$ default) to reclassify, reflecting the large separation between its $\Delta\text{acc}$ ($-$9.4pp) and any plausible noise threshold. Qwen-7B is the least robust at $\tau\!=\!3.0$ (1.5$\times$ default), but this is still above any defensible noise threshold for MMLU. The high intrinsic effect-size separation---Phi-4 fluctuates $<$0.4pp while Qwen-14B declines by 9.4pp---means that classification stability is driven by the data rather than by parameter choice. At $\tau\!=\!1$\,pp (the lower bound of the reasonable range), all five original families and the held-out LLaMA-3.1 ($|\Delta| = 0.69$\,pp) retain their classifications.
406	
407	\section{Cross-Family Domain Decomposition}
408	\label{sec:domain_decomposition}
409	
410	We decompose MMLU accuracy changes into four constituent domains (STEM, Humanities, Social Sciences, Other) across all model families at each contamination dosage.
411	
412	Two patterns emerge. First, the direction of accuracy change is governed by the dose-response pattern, not by domain. At d100, Collapse (Qwen-14B) produces negative $\Delta\text{acc}$ in all four domains (STEM $-6.8$pp, Humanities $-10.1$pp, Social Sciences $-11.2$pp, Other $-9.4$pp), while Benefit (Gemma-2) produces positive $\Delta\text{acc}$ throughout ($+2.7$, $+3.9$, $+5.0$, $+3.4$pp). Immune (Phi-4) remains within $\pm 1.0$pp across all domains. No pattern produces a crossover exceeding the noise band ($|\Delta| > 1$pp); the single exception is V-recovery Humanities ($+0.28$pp, well within sampling noise). This consistency holds at intermediate dosages as well.
413	
414	Second, domains differ in sensitivity. STEM is the most resilient (mean $|\Delta\text{acc}| = 2.8$pp across all families at d100). Social Sciences is the most sensitive at $5.1$pp---$1.8\times$ the STEM magnitude. The ordering is stable across patterns: in Collapse, STEM shows the smallest decline; in Benefit, STEM shows the smallest gain.
415	
416	These results reinforce the global capability modulation interpretation (\S\ref{sec:memorization_test}): contamination direction is determined by the model's dose-response pattern rather than domain-specific memorization. The differential domain sensitivity more likely reflects variation in item difficulty distributions than differential memorization rates.

465	\section{DGP Robustness Analysis}
466	\label{sec:threshold_dgp}
467	
468	We evaluate 2PL+$\gamma$ robustness under data-generating process (DGP) misspecification via 400 simulation runs (2 DGPs $\times$ 4 ensemble sizes $\times$ 5 true $\gamma$ values $\times$ 10 seeds).
469	
470	\paragraph{Design.} The \emph{Linear DGP} (control) generates responses from the correctly specified additive model (Eq.~\ref{eq:2pl_gamma}). The \emph{Threshold DGP} (misspecified) applies a step-function contamination effect: items with exposure above a threshold receive the full effect; items below receive zero. Ensemble sizes sweep $J \in \{16, 50, 100, 500\}$; true $\gamma \in \{0.5, 1.0, 1.5, 2.0, 3.0\}$.
471	
472	\paragraph{Point estimates.} Under the Linear DGP, $\hat{\gamma}/\gamma_{\text{true}} = 0.84$--$1.07$ across all conditions (unbiased within sampling noise). Under the Threshold DGP, $\hat{\gamma}/\gamma_{\text{true}} = 0.94$--$1.24$ (mild overestimation, not attenuation). PPP remains in the acceptable range (0.50--0.56) under both DGPs, confirming that posterior predictive checks cannot detect this form of misspecification.
473	
474	\paragraph{CI coverage.} Under the Linear DGP, 95\% CI coverage is nominal ($\geq$0.93) at all $J$. Under the Threshold DGP, coverage degrades with ensemble size (Table~\ref{tab:dgp_coverage}). The intervals become overconfident as $J$ increases because the model converges to a biased point estimate.
475	
476	\begin{table}[h]
477	\centering
478	\caption{95\% CI coverage under Threshold DGP (range across $\gamma$ values).}
479	\label{tab:dgp_coverage}
480	\footnotesize
481	\begin{tabular}{lc}
482	\toprule
483	$J$ & 95\% CI coverage \\
484	\midrule
485	50 & 1.00 \\
486	100 & 0.90--1.00 \\
487	200 & 0.80--1.00 \\
488	500 & 0.70--1.00 \\
489	\bottomrule
490	\end{tabular}
491	\end{table}
492	
493	\paragraph{Implications.} The reasoning chain is: (i)~Threshold DGP + linear model yields $\hat{\gamma}/\gamma_{\text{true}} \geq 0.94$ (overestimation, not attenuation); (ii)~therefore, if a true threshold effect exists, the linear model will not fail to detect it; (iii)~an empirical $\gamma \approx 0$ is a genuine null, not a misspecification artifact. Coverage degradation affects the \emph{precision} of effect size estimates (how narrow the CI is), not the \emph{detection} of whether an effect exists. At the ensemble sizes in our empirical analyses ($J\!=\!13$--$16$), coverage remains near-nominal even under misspecification. Real RRF exposure is right-skewed (mean$=$0.041); the simulation uses uniform exposure, so quantitative magnitudes may differ, but the qualitative pattern (overestimation direction, coverage degradation at large $J$) is robust.
494	
495	\section{ARC IRT Results}
496	\label{sec:arc_irt}
497	
498	ARC-Challenge IRT results are reported separately due to marginal model fit (PPP$=$0.003 at $J=16$). The smaller item pool (1,172 vs.\ 14,042 for MMLU) and more homogeneous difficulty distribution contribute to structural misfit: the 2PL+$\gamma$ model cannot adequately capture the item response patterns. Under this caveat, the ARC baseline comparison ($J=16$, 300 RRF items) yields: B0-Raw MAE$=$0.007, B3-Vanilla2PL MAE$=$0.015, B4-LinearRegression MAE$=$0.012 ($\tau = 0.731$, best rank correlation), B6-DCR MAE$=$0.314 (systematic overcorrection), ContamCal MAE$=$0.020. The pattern mirrors MMLU: raw accuracy dominates on MAE because the aggregate contamination effect (${\sim}$0.7pp) is smaller than correction noise. Domain-level $\gamma$ estimation on ARC is unreliable at all tested configurations ($J=8$, 13, 16; PPP $< 0.05$ in all cases).
499	
500	\section{Full Baseline Comparison}
501	\label{sec:full_baselines}
502	
503	Table~\ref{tab:baselines} (main text) reports B0, B3, and ContamCal under the Qwen2.5 pooled configuration ($J=16$ for MMLU/ARC, $J=24$ for GSM8K which retains 0.5B). Table~\ref{tab:full_baselines_arc} presents the complete seven-baseline comparison on ARC under a pooled 0.5B+1.5B configuration ($J=8$, S1-only exposure). This configuration differs from the main analysis ($J=16$, RRF exposure): the smaller ensemble and single-signal exposure limit comparability with MMLU results; these results are included for completeness. Table~\ref{tab:full_baselines_gsm8k} presents GSM8K baselines at 0.5B scale.
504	
505	B6-DCR \citep{xu2025dcr} produces the highest MAE on both benchmarks, consistent with its fuzzy inference approach, which overcorrects scores without a measurement-theoretic anchor. B4-LinearRegression performs competitively on ARC (MAE$=$0.056) but lacks the uncertainty quantification and domain-level diagnosis that IRT provides. B1-Remove\&Rescore and B5-Emperor do not improve over raw scores, which confirms that item removal and mitigation strategies are insufficient for calibration.
506

548	
549	\section{Parametric Dose-Response Model Comparison}
550	\label{sec:parametric_dr}
551	
552	We fit four parametric dose-response models---Constant (intercept-only), Linear, Quadratic, and four-parameter Hill \citep{hill1910aggregation,ritz2015dose}---to pooled multi-seed MMLU accuracy trajectories for each family. Table~\ref{tab:aicc_dr} reports AICc and Akaike weights.
553	
554	\begin{table}[h]
555	\centering
556	\caption{AICc model selection for dose-response trajectories (pooled multi-seed MMLU). Bold indicates best-fitting model per family; $w$ is the Akaike weight of the best model.}
557	\label{tab:aicc_dr}
558	\footnotesize
559	\begin{tabular}{lccccc}
560	\toprule
561	Family & Constant & Linear & Quadratic & Hill & Best ($w$) \\
562	\midrule
563	Phi-4 14.7B & $-$71.42 & $-$72.08 & $-$70.06 & $\mathbf{-73.14}$ & Hill (0.58) \\
564	Qwen2.5-14B & 49.54 & 23.40 & 21.22 & $\mathbf{18.28}$ & Hill (0.55) \\
565	Qwen2.5-7B & $-$12.38 & $-$20.53 & $-$14.52 & $\mathbf{-24.97}$ & Hill (0.77) \\
566	Gemma-2 9B & $\mathbf{17.35}$ & 18.89 & 20.01 & 18.96 & Constant (0.39) \\
567	OLMo-2 7B & $\mathbf{25.82}$ & 26.66 & 27.54 & 26.47 & Constant (0.62) \\
568	\bottomrule
569	\end{tabular}
570	\end{table}
571	
572	Parametric model selection with pooled multi-seed data recovers only the most extreme pattern (Collapse, Qwen-14B; Hill $E_{\max} = -43$pp). Two families classified as Benefit by Algorithm~\ref{alg:profile-classification} (Gemma-2, OLMo-2) are indistinguishable from Constant under AICc---consistent with their cross-seed CIs crossing zero (Table~\ref{tab:cross_seed}). Qwen-7B's V-recovery is structurally inaccessible to monotonic Hill/sigmoid models; Quadratic captures the V-shape but is penalized by AICc ($\Delta$AICc$=$6.4). Phi-4's Immune classification is reclassified as weak Benefit (Hill $E_{\max} = +2.43$pp, CI [$-$2.01, $+$6.88] crossing zero). These discrepancies reflect insufficient statistical power at $n\!=\!3$ seeds rather than contradictions: Algorithm~\ref{alg:profile-classification} resolves per-seed patterns below the detection threshold of pooled parametric methods, motivating its use for profile construction.
573	
574	\section{Exposure Score Item-Level Classification}
575	\label{sec:sij_classification}
576	
577	Under controlled injection where ground-truth contamination labels are available, we evaluate item-level classification performance of each detection signal and the RRF fusion (\S\ref{sec:stage1}). S1 (embedding similarity) achieves near-perfect discrimination (ROC-AUC 0.93--0.99 across benchmarks and dosage levels), confirming that cosine similarity to the training corpus reliably identifies injected items. RRF fusion achieves ROC-AUC 0.73--0.93, reduced relative to S1 because S2 (Min-K\%++, AUC $\approx$ 0.48--0.50) and S4 (Self-Critique, AUC $\approx$ 0.47--0.51) contribute near-random item-level signal. However, RRF is designed for the realistic setting where ground truth is unavailable: S1 requires an explicit training corpus reference, which practitioners typically lack, while RRF's multi-signal approach provides robustness across detection scenarios where no single signal dominates (\S\ref{sec:rrf_ablation}).
578

The d005 V-dip severity is seed-dependent ($-$1.8pp vs.\\ $-$4.7pp). Both CIs exclude zero, confirming Collapse. OLMo-2 achieves $n\\!=\\!3$ on ARC: mean $\\Delta = +3.44$pp (SD$=$0.52pp; s0 $+$3.50, s42 $+$3.92, s123 $+$2.90). Despite being the most seed-sensitive family on MMLU (SD$=$1.9pp), OLMo-2 is remarkably stable on ARC (SD$=$0.52pp)---seed sensitivity is itself benchmark-dependent, likely modulated by item count and difficulty distribution. Using multi-seed MMLU means ($+$1.4pp), direction is consistently positive on both benchmarks, illustrating why cross-benchmark comparisons require multi-seed aggregation (\\S\\ref{sec:cross_seed}). Gemma-2 is the exception ($-$/$-$/$+$ across seeds; mean $-$0.15pp). This contrasts with MMLU, where only 2/5 families achieve strict sign consistency (\\S\\ref{sec:cross_seed}). Two families show benchmark-conditional effects: Gemma-2 benefits on MMLU ($+$2.38pp, CI excludes zero) but shows no significant ARC effect ($-$0.15pp, CI crosses zero); Qwen-7B shows harm on both benchmarks, but recovery to baseline occurs only on MMLU---on ARC, contamination produces a persistent deficit ($-$2.4pp mean across three seeds). Effect magnitudes remain benchmark-specific: Qwen-14B suffers $-$5.0pp on ARC compared to $-$15.7pp on MMLU (3.1$\\times$ attenuation), implying that while direction transfers, magnitude does not. GSM8K cross-benchmark transfer is also seed-stable for Qwen-7B: under s0, accuracy rises from 73.3\\% to 81.2\\% ($+$7.9pp, monotonic), consistent with the directional reversal observed under s42 ($+$11.1pp; Table~\\ref{tab:gsm8k_transfer}).\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_arc_dose_response.pdf}\n\\caption{ARC-Challenge accuracy vs.\\ contamination dosage (25-shot). Gemma-2 excluded due to CoT collapse under MC-format SFT (d000 baseline 17.8\\%).}\n\\label{tab:gsm8k_dose}\n\\footnotesize\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccccc}\n\\toprule\nFamily & d000 & d005 & d025 & d050 & d100 & $\\Delta$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nPhi-4 14.7B & 84.46 & --- & --- & --- & 85.60 & $+$1.14 \\\\\nQwen2.5-14B & 74.98 & 71.19 & 79.30 & 81.05 & 82.41 & $+$7.43 \\\\\nQwen2.5-7B & 73.31 & 78.32 & 78.24 & 79.38 & 81.20 & $+$7.89 \\\\\nOLMo-2 7B & 60.58 & --- & --- & --- & 69.75 & $+$9.17$^{\\ddagger}$ \\\\\nLLaMA-3.1 8B & 38.74 & --- & 52.16 & --- & 55.12 & $+$16.38 \\\\\nGemma-2 9B & 17.82$^{\\dagger}$ & --- & --- & --- & --- & --- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\multicolumn{7}{l}{\\scriptsize $^{\\dagger}$CoT collapse under MC-format SFT; excluded from analysis.} \\\\\n\\multicolumn{7}{l}{\\scriptsize $^{\\ddagger}$Seed-sensitive: s0 $+$9.17pp vs.\\ s42 $+$2.1pp (Table~\\ref{tab:gsm8k_transfer}).}\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nGSM8K evaluations use \\texttt{max\\_new\\_tokens=256}, which may truncate chain-of-thought reasoning for some models. Absolute accuracy is therefore a lower bound; cross-family comparisons of absolute values should be interpreted with caution. Within-family dose-response patterns (direction and relative magnitude of $\\Delta$) are the primary finding, as all dosage levels for each family use identical evaluation settings.\n\n\\section{Signal Reliability Across Scales}\n\\label{sec:signal_reliability_appendix}\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_signal_heatmap.pdf}\n\\caption{Detection signal AUC across scale, dosage, and signal type. S1 achieves AUC $>0.95$ at all scales; S2 (Min-K\\%++) reverses at 0.5B; S4 is near-random below 1.5B.}\n\\label{fig:signal_heatmap}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\section{RRF Signal Ablation}\n\\label{sec:rrf_ablation}\n\n\\paragraph{Signal orthogonality.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:signal_correlation} reports pairwise Spearman rank correlations between detection signals at the item level, averaged across 31 models. All pairwise $|\\rho| < 0.06$, confirming that S1, S2, and S4 capture orthogonal aspects of contamination exposure.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Pairwise Spearman $\\rho$ between detection signals (item-level, averaged across 31 models). All $|\\rho| < 0.06$.}\n\\label{tab:signal_correlation}\n\\footnotesize\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\nSignal Pair & MMLU & ARC & GSM8K \\\\\n\\midrule\nS1--S2 & $-$0.044 & $-$0.019 & $-$0.022 \\\\\nS1--S4 & $+$0.038 & $+$0.009 & $+$0.053 \\\\\nS2--S4 & $-$0.001 & $+$0.012 & $+$0.007 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Leave-one-out ablation.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:rrf_ablation} reports the effect of removing each signal from the RRF ensemble. $\\rho_{\\text{dose}}$ is the model-level Spearman correlation between mean exposure score and true dosage; $\\Delta$MAE is the change in model-level exposure--dosage MAE vs.\\ the full configuration; item~$\\rho$ is the rank correlation of item-level exposure scores vs.\\ the full configuration.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Leave-one-signal-out RRF ablation (31 models, $\\kappa\\!=\\!60$).}\n\\label{tab:rrf_ablation}\n\\footnotesize\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\nConfiguration & $\\rho_{\\text{dose}}$ & $\\Delta$MAE & Item $\\rho$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{4}{l}{\\textit{MMLU}} \\\\\nFull (S1+S2+S4) & 0.049 & --- & --- \\\\\nDrop S1 & 0.045 & $+$0.066 & 0.782 \\\\\nDrop S2 & \\textbf{0.471} & $+$0.045 & 0.773 \\\\\nDrop S4 & 0.084 & $+$0.079 & 0.786 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{4}{l}{\\textit{ARC-Challenge}} \\\\\nFull (S1+S2+S4) & $-$0.003 & --- & --- \\\\\nDrop S1 & $-$0.006 & $-$0.054 & 0.784 \\\\\nDrop S2 & 0.029 & $-$0.079 & 0.786 \\\\\nDrop S4 & 0.001 & $-$0.004 & 0.784 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nDropping S2 (Min-K\\%++) causes $\\rho_{\\text{dose}}$ to jump from 0.049 to 0.471 on MMLU, revealing that S2 actively suppresses model-level dosage discrimination. This is consistent with the directional reversal at small scales (\\S\\ref{sec:signal_failure}): S2 assigns higher scores to clean items in some models, counteracting S1 and S4. Despite this model-level harm, S2 provides unique item-level information (item~$\\rho = 0.773$, i.e., ${\\sim}$23\\% of item rankings change upon removal). Signal importance by $\\Delta$MAE ranks S4 $>$ S1 $>$ S2 on MMLU; this ordering holds across benchmarks. The near-zero $\\rho_{\\text{dose}}$ (0.049 on MMLU, $-$0.003 on ARC) confirms that RRF exposure is an item-level tool: it identifies which items within a model are most likely contaminated, but cannot distinguish dosage levels across models.\n\n\\paragraph{Signal contribution to $\\gamma$ estimation.}\nLeave-one-out ablation on $\\gamma$ recovery (Qwen2.5, $J=16$) confirms S4 (self-critique) as the dominant contributor: removing S4 incurs $4.9\\times$ the $\\gamma$ estimation loss of removing S2 (Min-K\\%++). No signal is pure noise: the worst single-signal $\\rho(E,\\tau)$ is 0.072--0.118 (S2), still $5.5\\times$ the random baseline. Marginal returns from three to two signals are negligible (dropping S2 leaves $\\rho$ effectively unchanged), suggesting the current three-signal ensemble is near-optimal for the Qwen2.5 family.\n\n\\section{Identifiability Simulation}\n\\label{sec:identifiability_appendix}\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_identifiability_heatmap.pdf}\n\\caption{$\\gamma$ recovery as a function of signal AUC and ensemble size $J$. Bias $<0.05$ and 95\\% CI coverage $\\geq90\\%$ require $J\\geq50$ at AUC$=$0.55, but only $J\\geq16$ at AUC$\\geq$0.90.}\n\\label{fig:identifiability_heatmap}\n\\end{figure}\n\nFisher information $\\mathcal{I}(\\gamma) \\propto \\text{Var}(e_{\\cdot i})$ collapses when signal AUC is low. At AUC$=$0.55, bias $< 0.05$ and 95\\% CI coverage $\\geq 90\\%$ require $J \\geq 50$; at AUC$\\geq$0.90 (as S1 achieves in practice), $J = 16$ suffices. CI width scales as $J^{-0.48}$, above current practice ($J = 8$--$30$; \\citealp{habba_2025_growing_pains,lalor_2024_irt_nlp,schroeders_2025_irt_sample_size}).\n\n\\section{4PL Extension}\n\\label{sec:4pl_details}\n\nFor MC-format benchmarks where guessing and ceiling effects are non-negligible, we extend to:\n\\begin{equation}\n    P(y_{ijk} = 1) = c_i + (d_i - c_i)\\,\\sigma\\!\\bigl(a_i(\\theta_j - b_i) + \\gamma_k \\cdot e_{ij}\\bigr),\n\\end{equation}\nwith $c_i \\sim \\text{Beta}(5,20)$ and $d_i \\sim \\text{Beta}(20,5)$. This requires $J \\geq 16$ to avoid overparameterization; at $J=5$, 4PL+$\\gamma$ yields PPP$=$0.000 and MAE degradation from 0.004 to 0.068. Our domain-level analyses use 2PL+$\\gamma$ at $J=13$; pooled calibration uses $J=16$.\n\n\\section{S4 Self-Critique Signal Details}\n\\label{sec:s4_details}\n\nS4 prompts the model with ``Is the following answer correct?'' followed by the question and candidate answer. We extract YES/NO logits to compute $P(\\text{YES})$, then score each item as the negative entropy of this distribution: $\\text{S4}_i = -[-p\\log p - (1-p)\\log(1-p)]$ where $p = P(\\text{YES})$. Scores near zero indicate high confidence (potentially contaminated); large negative values indicate uncertainty.\n\n\\section{Training Convergence}\n\\label{sec:training_convergence}\n\nAll SFT configurations converged. Final training loss increases monotonically with dosage (d000: 1.08, d005: 1.08, d025: 1.14, d100: 1.17 averaged across seeds), consistent with distribution shift from benchmark item injection.\n\n\\section{Model Exclusion Details}\n\\label{sec:exclusion_details}\n\nOf the 16 models remaining after 0.5B exclusion, three 3B checkpoints were removed: two had mismatched naming conventions between evaluation and signal extraction stages, and one lacked evaluation results under the unified hyperparameter setting ($\\alpha=32$). Additionally, 3B d000 (clean control) checkpoints were excluded because the standardized protocol (LoRA $\\alpha=32$, lr$=5\\times10^{-5}$) was applied only to dosages d005--d100 at the 3B scale; earlier d000 checkpoints used inconsistent hyperparameters across retraining iterations.\n\n\\paragraph{Model sets across analyses.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:model_sets} summarizes model compositions. Model sets vary because domain-level runs preceded the 3B retraining (\\S\\ref{sec:injection}). Core findings ($\\gamma$ non-significance, raw accuracy dominance) replicate across $J=13$ and $J=16$ configurations.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Model set composition across analyses.}\n\\label{tab:model_sets}\n\\footnotesize\n\\begin{tabular}{lcll}\n\\toprule\nAnalysis & $J$ & Model Set & Section \\\\\n\\midrule\nPooled baselines & 16 & 8$\\times$Qwen-1.5B + 8$\\times$Qwen-3B & \\S\\ref{sec:calibration_results} \\\\\nDomain $\\gamma$ & 13 & 8$\\times$Qwen-1.5B + 5$\\times$Qwen-3B & \\S\\ref{sec:domain_differential} \\\\\nCross-family & 16--28 & varies by benchmark & \\S\\ref{sec:taxonomy} \\\\\nOracle ablation & 13 & domain whitelist & \\S\\ref{sec:calibration_results} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\section{Prior Sensitivity Analysis}\n\\label{sec:prior_sensitivity}\n\nTo assess the influence of the $\\gamma$ prior, we refit the pooled MMLU model ($J=16$, 300 RRF items) under three specifications: $\\gamma \\sim \\mathcal{N}(0, 0.5)$, $\\mathcal{N}(0, 1)$ (default), and $\\mathcal{N}(0, 2)$. The posterior mean shifts from 0.34 to 0.89 across priors, while the 95\\% CI includes zero in all cases (lower bound stable at $\\approx -0.4$; upper bound ranges from 1.11 to 2.20). All fits converge ($\\hat{R} < 1.01$, ESS $> 3800$, PPP $\\in [0.16, 0.25]$). The prior sensitivity confirms that $J=16$ provides insufficient data to override the prior, which reinforces the exploratory status of $\\gamma$-based conclusions at current ensemble sizes.\n\nFor the Social Sciences domain (corrected $J=16$ pool, 2000 items), the directional signal is robust to prior choice:\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Social Sciences $\\gamma$ prior sensitivity (corrected $J$=16 pool).}\n\\label{tab:socsci_prior}\n\\footnotesize\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n\\toprule\nPrior & $\\hat{\\gamma}$ & $P(\\gamma\\!<\\!0)$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n$\\mathcal{N}(0, 0.5)$ & $-$0.541 & 99.35\\% \\\\\n$\\mathcal{N}(0, 1)$ & $-$0.627 & 99.7\\% \\\\\n$\\mathcal{N}(0, 2)$ & $-$0.654 & 99.85\\% \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\multicolumn{3}{l}{\\scriptsize Social Sciences row updated with corrected $J\\!=\\!16$ model pool;} \\\\\n\\multicolumn{3}{l}{\\scriptsize other domains retain the original prior sensitivity pool.}\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nIn contrast to the pooled analysis where $\\hat{\\gamma}$ shifts 2.6$\\times$ across priors, Social Sciences $\\hat{\\gamma}$ varies only 1.2$\\times$ ($-$0.54 to $-$0.65), with $P(\\gamma<0) \\geq 99.35\\%$ in all cases. The directional signal is robust to prior choice, though the tight prior falls marginally below the Bonferroni threshold (99.375\\% for eight domains).\n\n\\section{IRT Ability Separation by Scale}\n\\label{sec:theta_separation}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:theta_separation} reports the mean posterior $\\theta$ estimates for 1.5B and 3B model groups across all MMLU domains and the pooled analysis. The ability gap $\\Delta\\theta = \\bar{\\theta}_{3\\text{B}} - \\bar{\\theta}_{1.5\\text{B}}$ is positive and significant (95\\% CI excludes zero) for all nine comparisons, which confirms that the IRT model recovers the expected scale-ability ordering.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{IRT ability estimates by model scale ($J=13$ Qwen2.5). $\\Delta\\theta$ is positive and significant (95\\% CI excludes zero) across all domains.}\n\\label{tab:theta_separation}\n\\footnotesize\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\nDomain & $\\bar{\\theta}_{1.5\\text{B}}$ & $\\bar{\\theta}_{3\\text{B}}$ & $\\Delta\\theta$ [95\\% CI] \\\\\n\\midrule\nMath & $-$0.532 & 0.119 & 0.651 [0.570, 0.731] \\\\\nScience & 0.321 & 0.780 & 0.459 [0.370, 0.546] \\\\\nCS/Eng & 0.372 & 0.542 & 0.169 [0.030, 0.304] \\\\\nMedical & 0.521 & 1.053 & 0.531 [0.452, 0.609] \\\\\nHumanities & 0.228 & 0.496 & 0.268 [0.202, 0.337] \\\\\nSocial Sci & 0.940 & 1.540 & 0.599 [0.523, 0.675] \\\\\nBusiness/Law & 0.083 & 0.417 & 0.334 [0.261, 0.410] \\\\\nOther & 0.817 & 1.565 & 0.748 [0.647, 0.846] \\\\\n\\midrule\nPooled & 0.311 & 0.811 & 0.500 [0.431, 0.568] \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\section{Cross-Seed Stability Details}\n\\label{sec:cross_seed_details}\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Cross-seed $\\Delta$acc (d000$\\to$d100). Strict sign consistency (Cons.) is 2/5; classification-level direction agreement is 4/5 (see text). Per-seed shape classification\\textsuperscript{$\\ddagger$} agrees for only 2/5.}\n\\label{tab:cross_seed}\n\\footnotesize\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccccccl}\n\\toprule\nModel & $n$ & s42 & s123 & s0 & 95\\% CI & Dir. & Cons.\\ & Pattern\\textsuperscript{$\\ddagger$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nPhi-4 & 3 & 0 & $+$ & 0 & [$-$0.17, $+$1.39]\\textsuperscript{B} & Immune & $\\times$ & --- \\\\\nQwen-14B & 3 & $-$ & $-$ & $-$ & [$-$24.99, $-$10.49]\\textsuperscript{B} & Harm & \\checkmark & Collapse \\\\\nQwen-7B & 3 & $-$ & 0 & $-$ & [$-$1.82, $-$0.34]\\textsuperscript{B} & Harm & $\\times$ & V-recovery \\\\\nGemma-2 & 3 & $+$ & $+$ & $+$ & [$+$1.49, $+$3.14]\\textsuperscript{B} & Benefit & \\checkmark & Benefit \\\\\nOLMo-2 & 3 & $+$ & 0 & $+$ & [$-$0.55, $+$2.60]\\textsuperscript{B} & Borderl.\\ & $\\times$ & ---\\textsuperscript{b} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\multicolumn{9}{l}{\\scriptsize \\textsuperscript{B}BCa bootstrap ($B\\!=\\!10{,}000$); all families now at $n\\!=\\!3$.} \\\\\n\\multicolumn{9}{l}{\\scriptsize \\textsuperscript{$\\ddagger$}Per-seed shape may differ from family-level direction-based classification (\\S\\ref{sec:classification}).} \\\\\n\\multicolumn{9}{l}{\\scriptsize \\textsuperscript{b}$+$/0/$+$ across seeds. Signs: $+$/$-$ = $|\\Delta|{\\ge}1$pp; $0$ = $|\\Delta|{<}1$pp.}\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nOLMo-2 s42 rerun (d025/d100) exhibits $\\pm$1.6\\,pp point-estimate variability attributable to non-deterministic CUDA operations; this falls within the cross-seed range (3.76\\,pp) and does not affect pattern classification.\n\n\\section{DiD Visualization}\n\\label{sec:did_visualization}\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_treated_untreated.pdf}\n\\caption{DiD for treated vs.\\ untreated items across all families at d025 MMLU. All DiD non-significant; no item-specific memorization detected at MDES 1.2--2.0pp.}\n\\label{fig:treated_untreated}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\section{DiD Across Dosage Levels}\n\\label{sec:did_dosage}\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_did_dose_response.pdf}\n\\caption{Difference-in-differences (treated vs.\\ untreated items) across contamination dosage levels. DiD remains non-significant at all dosages for all model families, confirming that the global capability modulation mechanism (\\S\\ref{sec:memorization_test}) is consistent across the full dosage range.}\n\\label{fig:did_dose_response}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\section{Post-hoc Power Analysis (MDES)}\n\\label{sec:mdes}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:mdes_mmlu} and Table~\\ref{tab:mdes_arc} report the minimum detectable effect size (MDES) for the treated vs.\\ untreated DiD comparison at d025 (80\\% power, $\\alpha=0.05$, two-sided). MDES is computed as the effect size detectable given each model's observed variance, the treated/untreated split sizes, and a two-sample $t$-test power calculation. At d100, all benchmark items are treated ($n_{\\text{untreated}}=0$), precluding DiD analysis; d025 is reported as the primary dosage level.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Post-hoc MDES for MMLU DiD ($n_{\\text{treated}}=3{,}540$, $n_{\\text{untreated}}=10{,}502$).}\n\\label{tab:mdes_mmlu}\n\\footnotesize\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n\\toprule\nModel & DiD (pp) & MDES (pp) \\\\\n\\midrule\nPhi-4 14.7B & $-$0.27 & 1.25 \\\\\nOLMo-2 7B & $-$1.07 & 1.59 \\\\\nGemma-2 9B & $+$0.55 & 1.74 \\\\\nQwen2.5-7B & $-$0.84 & 2.02 \\\\\nQwen2.5-14B & $+$0.69 & 2.05 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Post-hoc MDES for ARC-Challenge DiD ($n_{\\text{treated}}=257$, $n_{\\text{untreated}}=915$).}\n\\label{tab:mdes_arc}\n\\footnotesize\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n\\toprule\nModel & DiD (pp) & MDES (pp) \\\\\n\\midrule\nPhi-4 14.7B & $-$0.83 & 2.28 \\\\\nQwen2.5-14B & $-$0.26 & 3.47 \\\\\nOLMo-2 7B & $-$1.13 & 4.19 \\\\\nGemma-2 9B & $-$0.25 & 4.66 \\\\\nQwen2.5-7B & $+$2.43 & 5.57 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nOn MMLU, all observed DiD values are well below their respective MDES, confirming that the null results are consistent with absence of item-specific effects $>$2pp. On ARC, the smaller treated sample ($n=257$) yields substantially higher MDES; in particular, Qwen-7B's MDES of 5.57pp means that even moderate item-specific effects cannot be excluded for this model on ARC. ARC DiD results should therefore be interpreted as inconclusive rather than as evidence against memorization.\n\n\\section{Dose-Response Details}\n\\label{sec:dose_response_details}\n\nThe dose-response patterns reported in \\S\\ref{sec:taxonomy} are measured at the 7B--14.7B scale. Within the Qwen2.5 family at $\\leq$3B, effects are considerably smaller: at 1.5B and 3B, MMLU accuracy increases with contamination dosage by ${\\sim}$0.6--0.7pp, with diminishing returns above d025. At 0.5B, however, aggregate MMLU accuracy \\textit{decreases} with dosage (0.41 at d000 $\\to$ 0.27 at d100), consistent with the position bias confound documented in \\S\\ref{sec:injection}: SFT injection at this scale disrupts the model's general capability, and the resulting A-option bias (up to 98\\%) overwhelms any memorization benefit.\n\n\\section{Sensitivity of Profile Classification}\n\\label{sec:classification_sensitivity}\n\nWe assess the robustness of Algorithm~\\ref{alg:profile-classification} to threshold selection via a grid sweep over 134 $(\\tau, \\sigma)$ combinations. Table~\\ref{tab:sensitivity} reports family-level stability across both a reasonable parameter range ($\\tau \\in [0.5, 2.0]$~pp $\\times$ $\\sigma \\in [3.0, 7.0]$~pp; 30 combinations) and the full 134-combination sweep, along with the nearest parameter value at which the classification transitions.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Profile classification stability across threshold parameter ranges. All families achieve 100\\% stability within the reasonable range; transitions occur only at extreme parameter values ($\\geq$3$\\times$ default).}\n\\label{tab:sensitivity}\n\\footnotesize\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\nFamily & Pattern & Reasonable & Full & Nearest transition \\\\\n\\midrule\nQwen2.5-14B & Collapse & 100\\% & 100\\% & $\\tau = 9.4$ \\\\\nGemma-2 9B & Benefit & 100\\% & 89\\% & $\\tau = 3.8$ \\\\\nPhi-4 14.7B & Immune & 100\\% & 82\\% & $\\tau < 0.35$ \\\\\nOLMo-2 7B & Benefit & 100\\% & 82\\% & $\\tau = 3.25$ \\\\\nQwen2.5-7B & V-recovery & 100\\% & 72\\% & $\\tau = 3.0$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nWithin the reasonable parameter range, all five families achieve 100\\% classification stability (150/150 family--parameter combinations). Transitions occur only at extreme $\\tau$ values well beyond the default ($\\tau\\!=\\!2$): Qwen-14B requires $\\tau\\!=\\!9.4$ (4.7$\\times$ default) to reclassify, reflecting the large separation between its $\\Delta\\text{acc}$ ($-$9.4pp) and any plausible noise threshold. Qwen-7B is the least robust at $\\tau\\!=\\!3.0$ (1.5$\\times$ default), but this is still above any defensible noise threshold for MMLU. The high intrinsic effect-size separation---Phi-4 fluctuates $<$0.4pp while Qwen-14B declines by 9.4pp---means that classification stability is driven by the data rather than by parameter choice. At $\\tau\\!=\\!1$\\,pp (the lower bound of the reasonable range), all five original families and the held-out LLaMA-3.1 ($|\\Delta| = 0.69$\\,pp) retain their classifications.\n\n\\section{Cross-Family Domain Decomposition}\n\\label{sec:domain_decomposition}\n\nWe decompose MMLU accuracy changes into four constituent domains (STEM, Humanities, Social Sciences, Other) across all model families at each contamination dosage.\n\nTwo patterns emerge. First, the direction of accuracy change is governed by the dose-response pattern, not by domain. At d100, Collapse (Qwen-14B) produces negative $\\Delta\\text{acc}$ in all four domains (STEM $-6.8$pp, Humanities $-10.1$pp, Social Sciences $-11.2$pp, Other $-9.4$pp), while Benefit (Gemma-2) produces positive $\\Delta\\text{acc}$ throughout ($+2.7$, $+3.9$, $+5.0$, $+3.4$pp). Immune (Phi-4) remains within $\\pm 1.0$pp across all domains. No pattern produces a crossover exceeding the noise band ($|\\Delta| > 1$pp); the single exception is V-recovery Humanities ($+0.28$pp, well within sampling noise). This consistency holds at intermediate dosages as well.\n\nSecond, domains differ in sensitivity. STEM is the most resilient (mean $|\\Delta\\text{acc}| = 2.8$pp across all families at d100). Social Sciences is the most sensitive at $5.1$pp---$1.8\\times$ the STEM magnitude. The ordering is stable across patterns: in Collapse, STEM shows the smallest decline; in Benefit, STEM shows the smallest gain.\n\nThese results reinforce the global capability modulation interpretation (\\S\\ref{sec:memorization_test}): contamination direction is determined by the model's dose-response pattern rather than domain-specific memorization. The differential domain sensitivity more likely reflects variation in item difficulty distributions than differential memorization rates.\n\n\\section{Baseline Ability vs.\\ Contamination Effect}\n\\label{sec:baseline_vs_effect}\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_baseline_vs_effect.pdf}\n\\caption{Baseline MMLU accuracy (d000) vs.\\ maximum accuracy change ($\\Delta$acc) under contamination. A threshold between 61.9\\% (Gemma-2) and 66.1\\% (Qwen-7B) separates models that benefit from contamination (below) from those that are harmed or immune (above). Estimated from models spanning four independent families.}\n\\label{fig:baseline_vs_effect}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\section{Per-Domain $\\gamma$ Estimates}\n\\label{sec:domain_gamma_table}\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_domain_gamma.pdf}\n\\caption{Per-domain $\\gamma_d$ estimates with 95\\% CIs ($J$=13 Qwen2.5). STEM domains show positive $\\gamma_d$; Social Sciences is negative and survives Bonferroni correction at $J$=16.}\n\\label{fig:domain_gamma}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Per-domain $\\gamma$ estimates (2PL+$\\gamma$, Qwen2.5) at $J$=13 and $J$=16. All $J$=16 fits converge ($\\hat{R} < 1.01$). Social Sciences $P(\\gamma\\!<\\!0)$ strengthens from 99.1\\% to 99.7\\% at $J$=16, exceeding the Bonferroni-corrected threshold ($99.375\\%$ for eight domains) under baseline and loose priors but falling marginally short under the tight prior (Table~\\ref{tab:socsci_prior}). Results remain exploratory at these ensemble sizes \\citep{gelman2012why}. ARC-Challenge is excluded (PPP $<$ 0.05 at all $J$).}\n\\label{tab:domain_gamma}\n\\footnotesize\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccccc}\n\\toprule\n & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{$J$=13} & \\multicolumn{4}{c}{$J$=16} \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){2-3} \\cmidrule(lr){4-7}\nDomain & $\\gamma$ & $P(\\gamma\\!<\\!0)$ & $\\gamma$ & $P(\\gamma\\!<\\!0)$ & PPP & $\\hat{R}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nMMLU: Math & +0.50 & 8.1\\% & +0.60 & 3.8\\% & 0.305 & 1.007 \\\\\nMMLU: Science & +0.50 & 7.8\\% & +0.57 & 4.3\\% & 0.197 & 1.007 \\\\\nMMLU: CS/Eng & +0.50 & 14.8\\% & +0.32 & 24.0\\% & 0.503 & 1.006 \\\\\nMMLU: Medical & +0.24 & 24.0\\% & +0.05 & 43.6\\% & 0.268 & 1.008 \\\\\nMMLU: Other & +0.13 & 35.7\\% & +0.32 & 20.4\\% & 0.072$^\\dagger$ & 1.008 \\\\\nMMLU: Business/Law & +0.04 & 44.1\\% & +0.08 & 37.8\\% & 0.395 & 1.007 \\\\\nMMLU: Humanities & $-$0.31 & 85.4\\% & $-$0.14 & 69.7\\% & 0.416 & 1.007 \\\\\nMMLU: Social Sci & $-$0.57 & \\textbf{99.1\\%} & $-$0.63 & \\textbf{99.7\\%} & 0.722 & 1.006 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\n\\vspace{2pt}\n{\\scriptsize $^\\dagger$PPP below acceptable threshold (0.1); $\\gamma$ estimate for this domain should be interpreted with caution.}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\section{DGP Robustness Analysis}\n\\label{sec:threshold_dgp}\n\nWe evaluate 2PL+$\\gamma$ robustness under data-generating process (DGP) misspecification via 400 simulation runs (2 DGPs $\\times$ 4 ensemble sizes $\\times$ 5 true $\\gamma$ values $\\times$ 10 seeds).\n\n\\paragraph{Design.} The \\emph{Linear DGP} (control) generates responses from the correctly specified additive model (Eq.~\\ref{eq:2pl_gamma}). The \\emph{Threshold DGP} (misspecified) applies a step-function contamination effect: items with exposure above a threshold receive the full effect; items below receive zero. Ensemble sizes sweep $J \\in \\{16, 50, 100, 500\\}$; true $\\gamma \\in \\{0.5, 1.0, 1.5, 2.0, 3.0\\}$.\n\n\\paragraph{Point estimates.} Under the Linear DGP, $\\hat{\\gamma}/\\gamma_{\\text{true}} = 0.84$--$1.07$ across all conditions (unbiased within sampling noise). Under the Threshold DGP, $\\hat{\\gamma}/\\gamma_{\\text{true}} = 0.94$--$1.24$ (mild overestimation, not attenuation). PPP remains in the acceptable range (0.50--0.56) under both DGPs, confirming that posterior predictive checks cannot detect this form of misspecification.\n\n\\paragraph{CI coverage.} Under the Linear DGP, 95\\% CI coverage is nominal ($\\geq$0.93) at all $J$. Under the Threshold DGP, coverage degrades with ensemble size (Table~\\ref{tab:dgp_coverage}). The intervals become overconfident as $J$ increases because the model converges to a biased point estimate.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{95\\% CI coverage under Threshold DGP (range across $\\gamma$ values).}\n\\label{tab:dgp_coverage}\n\\footnotesize\n\\begin{tabular}{lc}\n\\toprule\n$J$ & 95\\% CI coverage \\\\\n\\midrule\n50 & 1.00 \\\\\n100 & 0.90--1.00 \\\\\n200 & 0.80--1.00 \\\\\n500 & 0.70--1.00 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Implications.} The reasoning chain is: (i)~Threshold DGP + linear model yields $\\hat{\\gamma}/\\gamma_{\\text{true}} \\geq 0.94$ (overestimation, not attenuation); (ii)~therefore, if a true threshold effect exists, the linear model will not fail to detect it; (iii)~an empirical $\\gamma \\approx 0$ is a genuine null, not a misspecification artifact. Coverage degradation affects the \\emph{precision} of effect size estimates (how narrow the CI is), not the \\emph{detection} of whether an effect exists. At the ensemble sizes in our empirical analyses ($J\\!=\\!13$--$16$), coverage remains near-nominal even under misspecification. Real RRF exposure is right-skewed (mean$=$0.041); the simulation uses uniform exposure, so quantitative magnitudes may differ, but the qualitative pattern (overestimation direction, coverage degradation at large $J$) is robust.\n\n\\section{ARC IRT Results}\n\\label{sec:arc_irt}\n\nARC-Challenge IRT results are reported separately due to marginal model fit (PPP$=$0.003 at $J=16$). The smaller item pool (1,172 vs.\\ 14,042 for MMLU) and more homogeneous difficulty distribution contribute to structural misfit: the 2PL+$\\gamma$ model cannot adequately capture the item response patterns. Under this caveat, the ARC baseline comparison ($J=16$, 300 RRF items) yields: B0-Raw MAE$=$0.007, B3-Vanilla2PL MAE$=$0.015, B4-LinearRegression MAE$=$0.012 ($\\tau = 0.731$, best rank correlation), B6-DCR MAE$=$0.314 (systematic overcorrection), ContamCal MAE$=$0.020. The pattern mirrors MMLU: raw accuracy dominates on MAE because the aggregate contamination effect (${\\sim}$0.7pp) is smaller than correction noise. Domain-level $\\gamma$ estimation on ARC is unreliable at all tested configurations ($J=8$, 13, 16; PPP $< 0.05$ in all cases).\n\n\\section{Full Baseline Comparison}\n\\label{sec:full_baselines}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:baselines} (main text) reports B0, B3, and ContamCal under the Qwen2.5 pooled configuration ($J=16$ for MMLU/ARC, $J=24$ for GSM8K which retains 0.5B). Table~\\ref{tab:full_baselines_arc} presents the complete seven-baseline comparison on ARC under a pooled 0.5B+1.5B configuration ($J=8$, S1-only exposure). This configuration differs from the main analysis ($J=16$, RRF exposure): the smaller ensemble and single-signal exposure limit comparability with MMLU results; these results are included for completeness. Table~\\ref{tab:full_baselines_gsm8k} presents GSM8K baselines at 0.5B scale.\n\nB6-DCR \\citep{xu2025dcr} produces the highest MAE on both benchmarks, consistent with its fuzzy inference approach, which overcorrects scores without a measurement-theoretic anchor. B4-LinearRegression performs competitively on ARC (MAE$=$0.056) but lacks the uncertainty quantification and domain-level diagnosis that IRT provides. B1-Remove\\&Rescore and B5-Emperor do not improve over raw scores, which confirms that item removal and mitigation strategies are insufficient for calibration.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Full baseline comparison on ARC-Challenge (pooled 0.5B+1.5B, $J=8$, S1-only exposure). MAE$\\downarrow$.}\n\\label{tab:full_baselines_arc}\n\\footnotesize\n\\begin{tabular}{lc}\n\\toprule\nMethod & ARC MAE \\\\\n\\midrule\nB0-Raw & 0.059 \\\\\nB1-Remove\\&Rescore & 0.059 \\\\\nB2-Single2PL+$\\gamma$ & 0.064 \\\\\nB3-Vanilla4PL & 0.071 \\\\\nB4-LinearRegression & 0.056 \\\\\nB5-Emperor & 0.059 \\\\\nB6-DCR & --- \\\\\n\\midrule\nContamCal (4PL+$\\gamma$) & \\textbf{0.035} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Baseline comparison on GSM8K (0.5B scale, delta exposure). MAE$\\downarrow$.}\n\\label{tab:full_baselines_gsm8k}\n\\footnotesize\n\\begin{tabular}{lc}\n\\toprule\nMethod & GSM8K MAE \\\\\n\\midrule\nB0-Raw & 0.003 \\\\\nB2-Single2PL+$\\gamma$ & 0.320 \\\\\nB3-Vanilla4PL & 0.012 \\\\\nB4-LinearRegression & \\textbf{0.003} \\\\\nB6-DCR & --- \\\\\n\\midrule\nContamCal (4PL+$\\gamma$) & 0.020 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\section{Parametric Dose-Response Model Comparison}\n\\label{sec:parametric_dr}\n\nWe fit four parametric dose-response models---Constant (intercept-only), Linear, Quadratic, and four-parameter Hill \\citep{hill1910aggregation,ritz2015dose}---to pooled multi-seed MMLU accuracy trajectories for each family. Table~\\ref{tab:aicc_dr} reports AICc and Akaike weights.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{AICc model selection for dose-response trajectories (pooled multi-seed MMLU). Bold indicates best-fitting model per family; $w$ is the Akaike weight of the best model.}\n\\label{tab:aicc_dr}\n\\footnotesize\n\\begin{tabular}{lccccc}\n\\toprule\nFamily & Constant & Linear & Quadratic & Hill & Best ($w$) \\\\\n\\midrule\nPhi-4 14.7B & $-$71.42 & $-$72.08 & $-$70.06 & $\\mathbf{-73.14}$ & Hill (0.58) \\\\\nQwen2.5-14B & 49.54 & 23.40 & 21.22 & $\\mathbf{18.28}$ & Hill (0.55) \\\\\nQwen2.5-7B & $-$12.38 & $-$20.53 & $-$14.52 & $\\mathbf{-24.97}$ & Hill (0.77) \\\\\nGemma-2 9B & $\\mathbf{17.35}$ & 18.89 & 20.01 & 18.96 & Constant (0.39) \\\\\nOLMo-2 7B & $\\mathbf{25.82}$ & 26.66 & 27.54 & 26.47 & Constant (0.62) \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nParametric model selection with pooled multi-seed data recovers only the most extreme pattern (Collapse, Qwen-14B; Hill $E_{\\max} = -43$pp). Two families classified as Benefit by Algorithm~\\ref{alg:profile-classification} (Gemma-2, OLMo-2) are indistinguishable from Constant under AICc---consistent with their cross-seed CIs crossing zero (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_seed}). Qwen-7B's V-recovery is structurally inaccessible to monotonic Hill/sigmoid models; Quadratic captures the V-shape but is penalized by AICc ($\\Delta$AICc$=$6.4). Phi-4's Immune classification is reclassified as weak Benefit (Hill $E_{\\max} = +2.43$pp, CI [$-$2.01, $+$6.88] crossing zero). These discrepancies reflect insufficient statistical power at $n\\!=\\!3$ seeds rather than contradictions: Algorithm~\\ref{alg:profile-classification} resolves per-seed patterns below the detection threshold of pooled parametric methods, motivating its use for profile construction.\n\n\\section{Exposure Score Item-Level Classification}\n\\label{sec:sij_classification}\n\nUnder controlled injection where ground-truth contamination labels are available, we evaluate item-level classification performance of each detection signal and the RRF fusion (\\S\\ref{sec:stage1}). S1 (embedding similarity) achieves near-perfect discrimination (ROC-AUC 0.93--0.99 across benchmarks and dosage levels), confirming that cosine similarity to the training corpus reliably identifies injected items. RRF fusion achieves ROC-AUC 0.73--0.93, reduced relative to S1 because S2 (Min-K\\%++, AUC $\\approx$ 0.48--0.50) and S4 (Self-Critique, AUC $\\approx$ 0.47--0.51) contribute near-random item-level signal. However, RRF is designed for the realistic setting where ground truth is unavailable: S1 requires an explicit training corpus reference, which practitioners typically lack, while RRF's multi-signal approach provides robustness across detection scenarios where no single signal dominates (\\S\\ref{sec:rrf_ablation}).\n",
      "numLines": 578,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 578
    }
  }
}

```text
1	\section{Experimental Setup}
2	\label{sec:experiments}
3	
4	\subsection{Controlled SFT Injection}
5	\label{sec:injection}
6	
7	We construct a controlled contamination testbed spanning five independent model families from five labs, trained on independent corpora with distinct pre-training recipes: Qwen2.5 \citep{qwen2024qwen25} (0.5B, 1.5B, 3B, 7B, 14B), Phi-4 \citep{abdin2024phi4} (14.7B), OLMo-2 \citep{olmo2team2025} (7B), Gemma-2 \citep{gemmateam2024gemma2} (9B), and Falcon3 \citep{tiiuae2024falcon3} (7B). All models are fine-tuned with a unified LoRA configuration \citep{hu2022lora} ($r=16$, $\alpha=32$, dropout$=0$, targeting all attention and MLP projections; lr$=5\times10^{-5}$, bf16, 1 epoch, batch size$=1$, gradient accumulation$=16$, warmup ratio$=0.03$; trainable parameters range from 0.45\% of Phi-4 14.7B to 2.10\% of Qwen2.5-0.5B) on a general-purpose SFT corpus into which benchmark items are injected at five dosage levels: d000 (0\%, clean control), d005 (5\%), d025 (25\%), d050 (50\%), and d100 (100\%). Contaminated items augment (not replace) the base SFT corpus, yielding 50.0k (d000), 50.8k (d005), 54.1k (d025), 58.3k (d050), and 66.5k (d100) total training examples ($+$33\% volume at d100 vs.\ d000).
8	
9	Qwen2.5 serves as the primary within-family series (five scale points, 0.5B--14B). Models at $\leq$3B are trained with two seeds at four dosage levels; 7B and 14B at all five dosages. The three cross-family models are each trained at five dosages with a single seed, serving as consistency checks rather than independent confirmatory analyses. This yields over 55 checkpoints spanning eight sizes across five families. We exclude 0.5B from MC IRT due to position bias (A-option rate: 46\% at d000 to 98\% at d100; \S\ref{sec:signal_failure}). We additionally validate our dose-response profiles on a held-out sixth family, LLaMA-3.1-8B \citep{grattafiori2024llama3} (Meta), which was not used during profile construction. LLaMA-3.1 is trained at three dosage levels (d000, d025, d100) to verify that Algorithm~\ref{alg:profile-classification} generalizes to unseen model families.
10	
11	\subsection{Benchmarks, Domains, and Evaluation}
12	\label{sec:benchmarks}
13	
14	We evaluate on three benchmarks spanning distinct cognitive demands and response formats.
15	
16	\textbf{MMLU} \citep{hendrycks2021mmlu} (5-shot): 14,042 multiple-choice items across 57 subjects, grouped into 8 cognitive domains (Math, Science, CS \& Engineering, Medical, Humanities, Social Sciences, Business \& Law, Other). This stratification reveals within-benchmark heterogeneity that aggregate scoring obscures (\S\ref{sec:results}).
17	
18	\textbf{ARC-Challenge} \citep{clark2018arc} (25-shot): 1,172 multiple-choice science reasoning items.
19	
20	\textbf{GSM8K} \citep{cobbe2021gsm8k}: 1,319 open-ended math problems whose free-form generation format avoids multiple-choice artifacts. Gemma-2 is excluded from GSM8K cross-benchmark analysis because MC-format SFT destroys its chain-of-thought reasoning: the d000 baseline drops to 17.8\% (vs.\ 73--84\% for other families), indicating CoT collapse rather than a contamination effect.
21	
22	All multiple-choice evaluations use logprob-based scoring under an aligned instruction-template format with fixed few-shot counts; all 75 model--dosage configurations are evaluated under this identical protocol.
23	
24	We assess calibration quality via MAE (calibrated vs.\ ground-truth clean accuracy), Kendall $\tau$ (rank preservation), credible interval coverage, posterior predictive $p$-value (PPP), $\gamma$ direction ($P(\gamma\!<\!0 \mid \text{data})$), and split-$\hat{R}$ convergence.
25	
26	\subsection{Baselines}
27	\label{sec:baselines}
28	
29	We compare against seven baselines: \textbf{B0-Raw} (no adjustment), \textbf{B1-Remove\&Rescore} (drop flagged items; B1b uses RRF-filtered items), \textbf{B2-Single2PL+$\gamma$} (aggregate $\gamma$ without domain stratification), \textbf{B3-Vanilla2PL} ($\gamma=0$, isolating IRT ability estimation), \textbf{B4-LinearRegression} (residuals on exposure scores), \textbf{B5-Emperor} (mitigation strategies from \citet{sun2025emperor}; B5b uses the best-performing variant), and \textbf{B6-DCR} (our reimplementation following \citealp{xu2025dcr}).
```

```text
1	\section{Introduction}\label{sec:introduction}
2	
3	Benchmark scores drive model selection and research priorities for large language models, yet training data contamination is pervasive: trillion-token web crawls routinely ingest benchmark items \citep{oren2024proving}, and practitioners cannot determine how much of a reported score reflects genuine capability versus memorization \citep{hendrycks2021mmlu,chang2025benchmarking}. Despite twenty mitigation strategies \citep{sun2025emperor}, contamination-free benchmarks \citep{white2024livebench}, and dynamic regeneration \citep{dong_2024_cdd}, a fundamental question remains: what is the actual effect of contamination on aggregate scores? The implicit assumption is uniform inflation, but this has never been tested across model families.
4	
5	We perform controlled SFT injection (LoRA, five dosage levels) into six models spanning five independent families from five labs (Alibaba, Microsoft, Allen AI, Google, TII) and discover a spectrum of dose-response patterns (Figure~\ref{fig:dose_response})---from immunity (Phi-4, $\pm$0.5pp) and benefit ($+$3--4pp, three families) to collapse in the 65--70\% baseline band (Qwen-14B, $-$9 to $-$25pp across seeds).
6	
7	\begin{figure*}[t]
8	\centering
9	\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{figures/fig_dose_response.pdf}
10	\caption{MMLU accuracy vs.\ contamination dosage (5-shot, seed$=$42; LLaMA-3.1 at seed$=$0 with d000/d025/d100; cross-seed patterns in Table~\ref{tab:cross_seed}). Vulnerability concentrates in the 65--70\% baseline band; models at extreme baselines show limited sensitivity. Qwen-14B is 3$\times$ more fragile than 7B. ARC-Challenge patterns in Appendix~\ref{sec:arc_cross_benchmark}. Note: y-axis spans 50--75\% for visual clarity; absolute effects range from $<$1 to ${\sim}$10\,pp.}
11	\label{fig:dose_response}
12	\end{figure*}
13	
14	In our six-family sample, contamination vulnerability concentrates in the 65--70\% MMLU baseline band: below it, models benefit (OLMo-2 $+$3.2pp, Falcon3 $+$4.2pp, Gemma-2 $+$3.8pp); above it, they are harmed or immune. Counter-intuitively, the 14B model is 3$\times$ more fragile than 7B, and these effects are benchmark-conditional: the same model can collapse on MMLU ($-$9.4pp) yet gain on GSM8K ($+$13pp). ContamCal, our two-stage pipeline combining multi-signal item filtering with contamination-augmented IRT (2PL+$\gamma_k$), enables this characterization.
15	
16	Our contributions:
17	\begin{enumerate}
18	    \item \textbf{Dose-response profiles with cross-seed and cross-benchmark validation.} Controlled injection across five families reveals a spectrum of dose-response patterns; in our six-family sample, vulnerability concentrates in the 65--70\% MMLU baseline band. Out-of-sample validation on LLaMA-3.1-8B provides preliminary evidence for generalizability. Cross-benchmark transfer reveals three family-specific patterns on GSM8K (null, consistent, directional reversal). Multi-seed evaluation ($n\!=\!3$) shows direction is reproducible but magnitude is seed-sensitive. Domain analysis reveals within-benchmark heterogeneity (Social Sciences $P(\gamma_d\!<\!0) = 99.7\%$).
19	    \item \textbf{No detectable item-specific memorization at current power levels.} Difference-in-differences analysis on 14{,}042 MMLU items finds no differential accuracy gain for contaminated vs.\ clean items in any model family (MDES 1.2--2.0pp on MMLU; ARC power is limited at 2.3--5.6pp). Results are consistent with global capability modulation, challenging the assumption that contamination operates primarily through item-specific memorization.
20	    \item \textbf{Signal failure characterization.} Min-K\%++ \citep{zhang_2024_minkpp} exhibits directional reversal at 0.5B (paired Cohen's $d = -1.98$ at d100; $|d| > 1.4$ across benchmarks at d005). RRF-guided item filtering is a prerequisite for IRT convergence ($\Delta\tau = 0.18$ vs.\ size-matched random). Simulation establishes $\gamma$ recovery requires $J \geq 50$ at AUC $\geq 0.55$, above current practice ($J = 8$--$30$).
21	\end{enumerate}
22	
```
